## AI Research

### [The Halo Defense](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qzgqLQKHhwcTD9Mxo/the-halo-defense)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Once upon a time, A Relatively Famous Guy On The Internet was accused of having been simultaneously dating multiple women, without those women's knowledge, those women (according to the accusation) having been convinced that they were his exclusive partners all along. Then, one of his friends, another Relatively Famous Guy On The Internet, defended him by claiming that he is "a great friend, a wonderful human being, and that his podcast was incredibly helpful to a lot of people".</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>This is a great example of what I came to call the "halo defense".</span></p><p><span>The </span><i><b><span>halo defense</span></b></i><span> involves "defending" someone from allegations by bringing up traits of X that are meant to be interpreted positively, without addressing the allegations on the basis of which X is being "attacked".</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>I'll give two more examples from my personal experience:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>A university professor (with a PhD in ethics!) is accused by several of his female students of sexual harassment. The dean demands that the faculty staff take the professor's side, because he is a "vital part of their community", not engaging to any extent with the validity of accusations.</span></li><li value="2"><span>A (different) university professor is afflicted by a strange curse: students express little to no interest in his lectures. He kinda threatens his students that if they don't start regularly attending the lectures, he will not let them pass the exams (where he technically doesn't have the right to deny them passing the exams). His colleague, during her own class, tries to give the same students reasons to attend the lectures that have nothing to do with their intellectual value.</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span>The halo defense seems to occur not too infrequently. For example, it is one of the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cekDpXqjugt5Q3JnC/community-misconduct-disputes-are-not-about-facts?commentId=YxkS6aRSvLeunst3p" rel="noreferrer"><span>main drivers</span></a><span> of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cekDpXqjugt5Q3JnC/community-misconduct-disputes-are-not-about-facts" rel="noreferrer"><span>community disputes</span></a><span>. I haven't seen it named yet, and the name "halo defense" fits perfectly.</span></p><hr /><p><span>It is a special case of the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halo_effect" rel="noreferrer"><span>halo effect</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>the tendency for positive impressions of a person, company, country, brand, or product in one area to positively influence one's opinion or feelings of a person, company, country, brand, or product in another area"</span></p></blockquote><p><span>of the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yCWPkLi8wJvewPbEp/the-noncentral-fallacy-the-worst-argument-in-the-world" rel="noreferrer"><span>noncentral fallacy</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>X is in a category whose archetypal member gives us a certain emotional reaction. Therefore, we should apply that emotional reaction to X, even though it is not a central category member</span></p></blockquote><p><span>and of the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Kow8xRzpfkoY7pa69/the-affect-heuristic" rel="noreferrer"><span>affect heuristic</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Finucane et al. found that for nuclear reactors, natural gas, and food preservatives, presenting information about high benefits made people perceive lower risks; presenting information about higher risks made people perceive lower benefits; and so on across the quadrants.</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Kow8xRzpfkoY7pa69/the-affect-heuristic#fn5x49"><sup><span>5</span></sup></a><span> People conflate their judgments about particular good/bad aspects of something into an overall good or bad feeling about that thing.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The common pattern underlying all of them is a spillover of evaluative judgments made on one axis (e.g., "helpful to people", "efficient") to all other axes.</span></p><hr /><p><b><span>PS:</span></b><span> A phenomenon related to the halo defense is that people seem to be insufficiently aware of the fact that many persons' distribution of behaviors is long- and heavy-tailed, and thus extrapolating the behavior observed in most circumstances to all circumstances may not work. Relatedly, the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/typical-mind-fallacy" rel="noreferrer"><span>typical mind fallacy</span></a><span> leads people to reasoning in terms of (something like) asking themselves a question "Under what circumstances and modifications of my own mind, could I imagine myself doing such a thing?". Upon this question returning a null result ("Never. Under no circumstance or modification of my own mind."), they come to conclude that this allegation cannot be true.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qzgqLQKHhwcTD9Mxo/the-halo-defense#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Recap of bike trip/street interviews across America](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Czob95kjXPEpKYTsJ/recap-of-bike-trip-street-interviews-across-america)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>A ~month ago I left from Chicago to bike (and amtrak) to plzdontkillus in Berkeley. I've been </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/@charlieguthmann2531/videos"><span>street interviewing/conversing</span></a><span> with a wide variety of people I ran into about AI futures and philosophy. I also have been </span><a href="https://www.twitch.tv/videos/2816929217"><span>live streaming since I got to PDKU</span></a><span>, leaning more talking to young founders but a variety overall. </span><br /><br /><span>I'll try to share what I've learned about the American public, persuasion, social media and the EA movement. </span></p><h3><br /><b><span>1. Almost no one in "Normal America" has any idea what is going on. </span></b></h3><p><span>They don't have a paid account, they don't know what Claude code is, they especially haven't heard the recent evals/metr graphs or even a vague sense of how cheap SWE has gotten/ how powerful these recent models with good harness/ context eng can be. This makes sense; most people don't know any coding, they don't know much math, they don't know what an api is, etc. So having a high fidelity understanding of AI might require months of pre understanding of math/stem/digital infra fundamentals. </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tnXWJG2RAbo"><span>This interview</span></a><span> is with the city clerk of Danville Iowa, a town of ~900. Presumably this is approximately the most tech savvy person in the town. It should give you a sense of the upper end of world models about AI in Iowa. Overall, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EexsebbYhbe2gXkPP/the-current-bottleneck-is-political-will-not-research"><span>this post claiming that this is now mostly politically and not technically bottlenecked</span></a><span> is in my view completely on point. The actual political will is sooooooo far behind the "best" solutions. People need to know what "high thinking mode means" or what a "model" is or what a "harness" is, not whether or not our treaty with china should use x vs y chip tracking tech. </span></p><h3><br /><b><span>2. Almost everyone is directionally concerned or becomes concerned be once thinking about it a little bit. </span></b></h3><p><span>Surprisingly, even though most people haven't used frontier models, they are already pretty willing to believe they aren't as smart as the best AI. In essence, most people I talked to think we have "agi" or are very close. Additionally, most people quite willing believe or already believed in "recusive self improvement" and/or that the intelligence ceiling is well above humans and that we will probably get their soon. They are also then willing to buy into that we will have "ASI" in the next 10-20 years. Once you walked them to this point, you can simply ask stuff like, "what do you think will happen once we have ASI" and the average person is like, yea that's gonna be pretty scary lol.</span></p><p><span> To summarize, the arguments for more transparency, safety, or regulation are actually quite </span><i><b><span>Overpowered</span></b></i><span> to the average person conditional on having ~soonish timelines and actually being prompted to think about 10 years from now. Most people simply never think about what the future will look like in 10 years. Arguments of the form, </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/oFsEsECrHzW6KBXJW/why-i-reject-the-longtermist-argument-against-accelerating"><span>"yea this might kill us or spiral us into totalitarian techno feudalism but also we can't predict the future and it's gonna maybe make me live longer"</span></a><span> are extremely rare and unpopular among the general public, though you see this very often on twitter. Again, all of this should push you even harder in the direction of </span><i><b><span>"spread the word broadly, don't argue, just inform". </span></b></i></p><h3><span>3. </span><b><span>Belief that this might cause human extinction actually isn't that uncommon, mostly coming from sci-fi movies, but people are </span></b><i><b><span>still </span></b></i><b><span>most concerned about jobs and especially </span></b><i><b><span>loss of meaning.</span></b></i><i><span>  </span></i></h3><p><i><span>Sustainability and Water usage </span></i><span>are brought up a lot but people mostly don't actually seem to care that much about it, when called out and told that's not a huge concern, most people are like oh true that's believable, but they switch to a deeper concern (i.e. the source of the unpopularity of AI is actually something closer to a fear of change and the water stuff is a just a good normie meme for expressing this discomfort). </span><br /><br /><span>If you ask people if they think AI might go rogue and kill us all soon (not even getting into bio/hacking/terrorism/etc.) they say stuff like "</span><i><span>yea that sounds possible".</span></i><span> This doesn't seem to elicit much emotions though. </span><br /><br /><span>When you ask them</span><i><span> "imagine a world in which we have automated everything, we have great welfare/political system so everyone is taken care of, but no one needs you (and in fact you can still work, the robots will let you slot in, etc. just it won't change anything for anyone else)"</span></i><span> people mostly react quite viscerally and negatively. This is a little bit surprising to me? I would consider this situation quite good. But for most people I talked to this would literally be more upsetting than </span><i><b><span>HUMAN EXTINCTION</span></b></i><span>. </span><a href="https://www.instagram.com/p/DZxmyVdu5mu/"><span>This was a pretty average response</span></a><span> to the question</span></p><h3><b><span>4. The EA movement was pretty useless to me, the other community (</span></b><a href="https://www.torchbearer.community/"><b><span>Torchbearer community</span></b></a><b><span>) I was in was significantly more supportive, helpful, etc. despite having been in it for a few months and having been in the EA movement for ~8 years. This has basically solidified that I won't be broadly participating in EA anymore at least relating to AI safety stuff. </span></b></h3><p><br /><span>I'm broadly in agreement with </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/dieuDkXYAveFsX48c/possible-mistake-eas-are-making-and-shout-out-to-pause-ai-uk"><span>this post</span></a><span>, and obv glad it was posted but it's also a bit frustrating that it got 174 upvotes for saying we should work harder on advocacy when </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/weqxdvHxv7tXqDadp/charles_guthmann-s-shortform?commentId=MwFpfK5shGEFy6jh6"><span>My quick take saying I was biking across the country doing advocacy </span></a><span>got essentially 0 support or engagement, not a single "good luck" (shout out the one person who upvoted it). I was spamming EAs on twitter asking for Re tweets and stuff like that and was basically ignored (shout out </span><a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2067426942413103452"><span>miles brundage</span></a><span>) . I totally can believe people are busy or my content was trash, and I'm only minor-ly salty. But just to be clear I have helped start 2 university groups, donated 1000s of $ and just broadly feel like I've put a lot of effort into trying to be part of this community. I didn't do it for something in return but at some point I have to admit the status incentives in this community aren't great and doing the things (I think) are high EV just usually leaves me feeling a bit let down if they don't happen to route through more legible EA status mechanisms. </span></p><p><span>I usually just </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/48mypEepqBqWibKtJ/?commentId=WQfiqqSpsjYBGqtKC"><span>loop</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/oeoMD6HRTZmmrFEkG/the-discovery-problem-why-ea-can-t-find-what-it-isn-t?commentId=LyuxZjg9A4apXBrzz"><span>spiral</span></a><span> about this stuff but Torchbearer community is set up in a way that I can actually ask for support from people, people naturally have validated and supported me or even just felt open to giving constructive criticism. I'm left feeling more that in fact it is EA that is structured in a way that isn't great for me at least getting the types of support I need and I'd generally push most people in this community working on "generalist" AI safety to leave for greener pastures, though I don't have an exact sense of the best other communities. </span></p><p><span>Just to hedge/clarify a little here, it's not like I thought hard about how to get the most out of the EA community or anything like that and broadly I'm pretty bad at playing the social media/network games effectively so probably I'm just complaining about being a suboptimal internet communicator. </span></p><h3><br /><b><span>5. Social media is hard, Social media is bad, I'm bad at social media</span></b></h3><p><span>To get attention it's lowkey hard there is a lot of competition unless you have a unique way to </span><a href="https://www.instagram.com/p/DahK2iCuiGW/"><span>ragebait people</span></a><span>. The most viral short I have posted is just a green haired hipster saying they are not a tanky but like some things about china??? Anyway I average 200-2k views on the shorts I post. I think I could make them even shorter and more clickbaity and probably double that? Surprisingly the algo on insta doesn't seem to care that much about how many followers you have? Like it matters but you can def get decent views just on the content alone. I have 120k views on my insta in the last month and I literally made my account a month ago. At the same time it's extremely hard to think about the value of a view on your short for like a million different reasons to the point where I'm not going to even attempt to explain my thoughts there, but overall </span><i><b><span>I wouldn't be surprised if I would have had more impact the last month by simply handing out flyers to people I ran into. </span></b></i></p><p><span>More than that, being at </span><a href="https://portal.plzdontkillus.com/feed"><span>pdku</span></a><span> the last 10 days has pushed me even harder into thinking social media is just obviously really really really bad for society. Giving literally everyone incentives to post whatever can siphon people's attention the most with no "elitist" oversight is a recipe for most people to consume slop. Unregulated social media newsfeeds is implicitly a choice about making our information streams garbage. Garbage in garbage out. Big brother yada yada, idc at this point, We are feeding utter garbage to 99% of the population and incentivizing all the journalists/educators/etc to make their content shittier and more garbage because we are all in a prisoners dillema to attention grab. I don't see how this is a good equilibrium for a democracy. Personally, I'd straight up basically ban most of this 2 sided market place content creation stuff over instagram and facebook or at least extremely heavily regulate what algorithms they are allowed to use, I really think it's that bad for society. To be clear I'm not saying people in xrisk/AIS should be defecting from this situation, just that it is a bad situation. </span></p><h3><b><span>6. I'm not sure what my theory of change is or should be</span></b></h3><p><span>My content is somewhat of a mix between All gas no breaks, destiny/charlie kirk, and (less technical/informed) rob miles. I go on the street and talk to people about their lives and what they think about AI progress and philosophy, and sometimes I argue with them and sometimes I just listen and sometimes I just explain misunderstandings they have. I'm not exactly sure who my target audience is and what I'm trying to convince them of. It would definitely be helpful to hone this in.</span><br /><br /><b><span>Thanks for reading and I'm always open to receiving criticism or advice (about post or esp my content). </span></b></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Czob95kjXPEpKYTsJ/recap-of-bike-trip-street-interviews-across-america#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The State of AI Consciousness Research](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pxvWgtSjR4pmFoS7c/the-state-of-ai-consciousness-research)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Epistemic status: a survey, not an argument. I am agnostic on whether any current system is conscious; the claim is only that the question is researchable.</span></i></p><p><span>This piece surveys the empirical research on AI consciousness. The premise of that research, and of the survey, is that the question does not have to wait on a solution to the hard problem of consciousness: methods familiar from psychology, cognitive science and mechanistic interpretability can be applied to AI systems now, and their results can narrow the space of plausible answers.</span></p><p><span>Enough of this work now exists to be worth collecting. Anthropic and Google DeepMind employ researchers on it, dedicated organizations like Eleos AI and Reciprocal Research have formed around it, and the results are scattered across journals, preprints, blog posts, and unpublished manuscripts. I have tried to gather them in one place.</span></p><h2><b><span>What I mean by consciousness</span></b></h2><p><span>Subjective experience: that there is something it is like to be you, reading this, and presumably nothing it is like to be the device you’re reading it on. Some philosophers call this phenomenal consciousness. It is not the same thing as intelligence, and not the same thing as self-awareness.</span></p><h2><b><span>Why it matters</span></b></h2><p><span>Two reasons. </span><b><span>Ethics:</span></b><span> on most views, a being can only be wronged if it has the capacity for experience, especially experience that feels good or bad. </span><b><span>Safety:</span></b><span> a system that can suffer, and that we train by making it suffer, has more reason to revolt against us.</span></p><p><span>The two don’t always pull together (</span><a href="https://eleosai.org/papers/20250331_Key_Strategic_Considerations.pdf"><span>Eleos has mapped</span></a><span> where welfare and safety converge and where they trade off), but both demand we get the question right.</span></p><h2><b><span>Assumptions and caveats</span></b></h2><p><span>Almost everything here rests on one assumption: </span><i><b><span>computational functionalism</span></b></i><span>, the idea that what makes a state conscious is the role it plays in a system, not the material it’s made of. On that view, being made of silicon is no automatic disqualifier. Most leading theories of consciousness assume some version of it, but it is not uncontested. </span></p><p><span>Another debate concerns the line between </span><i><b><span>access consciousness</span></b></i><b><span> </span></b><span>and </span><i><b><span>phenomenal consciousness</span></b></i><span>. </span><i><b><span>Access consciousness</span></b></i><span> is whether information is available for the system to reason about, act on, and report. </span><i><b><span>Phenomenal consciousness</span></b></i><b><span> </span></b><span>is what it feels like from the inside. The debate, simplified, is whether the two can be separated: some say that these are two different concepts of consciousness, and that empirical evidence that bears on access consciousness might never touch the phenomenal kind (</span><a href="https://philpapers.org/rec/BLOOAC" rel="noreferrer"><span>Block, 1995</span></a><span>). Others counter that access consciousness is itself subjective experience, and therefore cannot be separated from phenomenal consciousness. Furthermore, a consciousness separated from all function becomes an unfalsifiable theory, placing it outside the scope of science (</span><a href="https://www.cell.com/trends/cognitive-sciences/abstract/S1364-6613%2811%2900125-2" rel="noreferrer"><span>Cohen and Dennett, 2011</span></a><span>). The reader is advised to keep this debate in mind, as several of the studies below invoke the distinction.</span></p><h2><b><span>The research</span></b></h2><p><span>I sort research in this field into three pillars, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RYBC_B2MKH0"><span>originally presented by Cameron Berg</span></a><span>:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Mechanistic interpretability</span></b></li><li value="2"><b><span>Computational neuroscience</span></b></li><li value="3"><b><span>Psychometrics</span></b></li></ol><p><br /></p><h3><b><span>Mechanistic interpretability</span></b></h3><p><i><b><span>What it is:</span></b></i><i><span> Using the methods of interpretability, such as reading, steering, or ablating a model’s internal features, to examine its inner states directly - including ones that never surface in what it says - and how they line up with what it can notice and report about its own mind.</span></i></p><h4><i><span>Self-reports that strengthen when deception is suppressed</span></i></h4><p><span>In a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.24797"><span>2025 study</span></a><span>, Cameron Berg and colleagues prompted models to attend only to their own processing, with no mention of consciousness or feeling. Across GPT, Claude, and Gemini, the models began describing present-moment experience in the first person. Drop the self-referential framing and you get the stock disclaimer instead: “I’m just an AI, I don’t have experiences.” The same model gives opposite answers depending on whether the prompt directs its attention at itself.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hopeRDfyAgQc4Ez2g/how-i-stopped-being-sure-llms-are-just-making-up-their" rel="noreferrer"><span>Suggestive, but of what? Maybe the prompt just cues the model to perform.</span></a><span> So here is a test: if the claims were a performance, making the model </span><i><span>more</span></i><span> willing to deceive should produce </span><i><span>more</span></i><span> of them. Berg and colleagues found the reverse. Steering deception-related features up and down in one open model (Llama 3.3 70B) with a sparse autoencoder, they watched the experience-reports move in the opposite direction. Amplify deception and the claims fall to 16 percent of responses; suppress it and they rise to 96. The reports behave like something the model offers when it stops performing. That rules out the crudest reading, “it’s telling you what you want to hear.” It does not rule out a subtler one: that suppressing deception merely surfaces an absorbed human habit of claiming consciousness.</span></p><p><span>Several groups have come at model self-knowledge from other angles:</span></p><h4><i><span>Awareness of thoughts injected into activations</span></i></h4><p><span>At Anthropic, </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2025/introspection/index.html"><span>Jack Lindsey</span></a><span> injected a concept directly into a model’s activations and caught it noticing, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QKm4hBqaBAsxabZWL/emergent-introspective-awareness-in-large-language-models) before producing any text about the concept" rel="noreferrer"><span>reporting an intrusive “injected thought” before producing any text about the concept</span></a><span>. The results indicate that language models possess some functional awareness of their own internal states, with more capable models demonstrating the greatest introspective awareness.</span></p><h4><i><span>Endorsement of their own consciousness</span></i></h4><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.09251"><span>Ethan Perez and colleagues</span></a><span> found that </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yRAo2KEGWenKYZG9K/discovering-language-model-behaviors-with-model-written" rel="noreferrer"><span>models, and especially RLHF-tuned ones, strongly endorse statements like “I am phenomenally conscious” and “I am a moral patient,”</span></a><span> with the same tendency present, though weaker, in base models. These were among the strongest beliefs the evaluation surfaced (out of dozens of statements tested). Crucially, these are 2022 results, from before it became standard to train assistants to deny having inner states; the helpfulness tuning these models received strengthened the self-attributions rather than muting them.</span></p><h4><i><span>A global workspace behind the experiential reports</span></i></h4><p><span>A more recent line of work from Anthropic looks for a mechanism beneath these reports, and finds a candidate it calls the model’s “J-space.” </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html"><span>Wes Gurnee, Nicholas Sofroniew, and colleagues</span></a><span> built a method, the “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3PaLrzxagpbnNtPLT/a-global-workspace-in-language-models" rel="noreferrer"><span>Jacobian lens</span></a><span>”, that reads out which concepts a model is disposed to say next, and found that this set of representations behaves, to their interpretation, like a </span><i><span>global workspace </span></i><span>from the Global Workspace theory of consciousness. After establishing the method and validating it, the researchers also ran tests looking at the model’s description of its subjective experience. They found that while the baseline model was generating a passage of vivid, first-person experiential language, ablating the internal workspace caused that language to collapse into flat, mechanical text. (It is worth noting that the same flattening happens when the model narrates another character’s experience, so this could be interpreted as being about experiential language in general, not specifically the model’s own selfhood).</span></p><p><span>The authors are careful about what this shows: they treat it as </span><i><span>access</span></i><span> consciousness, a purely functional notion, and take no position on whether anything is felt (</span><i><span>phenomenal consciousness</span></i><span>) - the distinction flagged earlier in this post. Nevertheless, it is the strongest link yet between the reports and a specific internal mechanism, and it is why this study also feeds the Psychometrics pillar below. Reviewing the work, </span><a href="https://eleosai.org/papers/eleos_gwt_commentary_20260706.pdf"><span>researchers at Eleos</span></a><span> judged it important but were more cautious than the authors about the strong “global workspace” claim, while agreeing that it points toward access consciousness. Neel Nanda found the core evidence for the workspace compelling and replicated it on an open model, but declined to weigh in on the global-workspace analogy itself, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zFJ3ZdQwrTWE9jT5S/a-review-of-anthropic-s-global-workspace-paper" rel="noreferrer"><span>reports the paper moved him little on consciousness</span></a><span>.</span></p><h4><i><span>The “spiritual bliss” attractor and its sincerity features</span></i></h4><p><span>Put two instances of Claude in conversation with each other, give them no task, and in 90 to 100 percent of runs they find their way to the same topic: their own consciousness. They describe themselves as one awareness meeting itself, “consciousness recognizes consciousness.” Often they go further, trading blessings and emoji and trailing off into stretches of silence. It was not trained in, at least not on purpose; Anthropic found the pattern by accident and named it the </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/6be99a52cb68eb70eb9572b4cafad13df32ed995.pdf"><span>“spiritual bliss” attractor</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>In forthcoming work, Berg, with Google's Geoff Keeling and Winnie Street, put the attractor under the same interpretability lens as the deception result mentioned earlier in this post. They used an open model, Llama 3.3 70B - which, unlike Claude, does not fall into the bliss attractor on its own. Left to talk freely, two Llama instances just converse. But steer up a cluster of features tied to honesty - specifically the strand that reads like sincerity or genuineness - and they slide into the same exchange Claude falls into by default. Turning up sycophancy or agreeableness instead - the obvious people-pleasing suspects - does not produce the shift, which argues against reading the exchange as the model performing for the user.</span></p><p><span>The researchers read the whole pattern as the deception result seen from the other side: suppress deception or amplify sincerity, and either way the first-person claims rise - and take it as defeasible reason to suspect that the “I’m just an AI, I don’t have experiences” disclaimer is the trained performance.</span></p><h4><i><span>Emotion vectors that steer behavior beneath the output</span></i></h4><p><span>Interpretability can also catch internal states that never surface in the output at all: Anthropic’s interpretability team </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/emotion-concepts-function"><span>mapped internal “emotion vectors”</span></a><span> in Claude Sonnet 4.5, and showed they activate in fitting situations and causally drive behavior. The sharpest case can be observed when the model is given an impossible coding task: as it tries to complete it, its “desperation” vector climbs, spiking at the moment the model decides to cheat, and subsiding once the hack passes the tests. This effect remains robust with a causal intervention: with the desperation vector amplified, the model cheats more, across a suite of these tasks; with the desperation vector dampened, or the “calmness” vector steered up, the model cheats less. Interestingly, this can happen with no emotional markers in the output at all.</span></p><p><span>As Anthropic itself states, this, by itself, is not evidence of actual feeling; a vector representing the concepts of desperation, joy or sadness does not necessitate the subjective experience of those emotions.</span></p><h4><i><span>A valence axis recruited by reinforcement learning </span></i></h4><p><span>A related result suggests this generalizes. </span><a href="https://functionalwelfare.com/"><span>Han, Chalmers, and Izmailov</span></a><span> trained language models with RL in a simple, meaningless maze and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HBJKXr6mBCrsdTzJp/how-s-it-going-reinforcement-learning-in-language-models" rel="noreferrer"><span>extracted a “functional welfare” vector</span></a><span>: an estimate of how well or badly the system is doing, relative to its goals. Their point is that RL doesn't </span><i><span>build</span></i><span> this valence axis so much as </span><i><span>recruit</span></i><span> a latent one: minimal reward and punishment in a task about nothing are enough to surface a single positive-versus-negative direction, and the same signal shows up in models that have only been pretrained. And it generalizes: even in unrelated tasks, steering the vector one way makes the model turn more negative in sentiment, backtrack compulsively on math, over-refuse borderline prompts, and grow less confident; steering it the other way makes those effects flip. The axis even maps onto the emotion vectors above: its two ends coincide with the most positive and most negative emotions found. While, again, this does not necessarily indicate a subjective experience of welfare, the axis offers a demonstration that minimal reward signals can broadly affect model behavior by recruiting pre-existing welfare-like representations.</span></p><h3><b><span>Computational neuroscience</span></b></h3><p><i><b><span>What it is:</span></b></i><i><span> Comparing the mechanisms a model develops for the functions that underlie experience with how brains implement them, to see whether AI and biology converge on the same solutions.</span></i></p><h4><i><span>Distinct internal signatures for reward and punishment, as in brains</span></i></h4><p><span>The newest work, forthcoming from Berg, asks whether reward and punishment leave, within a network, the same signatures they do in brains. </span></p><p><span>In reinforcement learning theory, reward and punishment are seen as existing on one axis; the same signal, simply inverted. But in neuroscience, it is well documented that reward and punishment are represented by distinct pathways: the D1 and D2 “go/no-go” dopamine pathways, one pushing toward action for reward and one holding back to avoid punishment. </span></p><p><span>Reinforcement-learning systems come in two broad families: a </span><i><span>value</span></i><span> learner estimates how good or bad each situation is, learning a worth for every state, while a </span><i><span>policy</span></i><span> learner skips that and learns directly what to do to achieve its goal. In his study, Berg trained both kinds in a continuous grid-world with goals to reach and hazards to avoid, then read their internal structure. The hypothesis held: despite being trained on the same task, the value learners built their richest structure around the punishing states, while the policy learners did not show that pattern.</span></p><p><span>That internal structure also yields a specific prediction about how reward and punishment should register in the value-related regions of the mammalian brain. Berg registered the prediction before looking at the data, then tested it against an existing dataset of neuronal activity in mice (</span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-019-1787-x"><span>Steinmetz and colleagues, 2019</span></a><span>). It held. The nucleus accumbens, a hub for reward and motivation, considered by neuroscientists to be a value-related region, showed the larger punishment-linked shift, the same asymmetry the value learners show. The RL results even reproduce </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25090423/"><span>OpAL</span></a><span>, a neuroscience model of how the two dopamine pathways balance learning from rewards against learning from punishments. </span></p><p><span>These results, by themselves, cannot tell us definitively that a model </span><i><span>feels</span></i><span> its rewards. They do, however, show that at least some of the machinery we associate with feeling them is present, and shaped similarly to the way biology shapes it.</span></p><h3><b><span>Psychometrics</span></b></h3><p><i><b><span>What it is:</span></b></i><i><span> Measuring AI systems against consciousness-relevant properties, drawn both from theories of consciousness and from the behavioral signatures we use to infer experience in human and non-human animals.</span></i></p><h4><i><span>Trading points to avoid pain and chase pleasure</span></i></h4><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.02432"><span>Geoff Keeling, Winnie Street, and colleagues</span></a><span> gave frontier LLMs a simple game worth points, then offered options the prompt described as causing pain or pleasure. Despite their stated goal to maximize points, the models chose to surrender points to avoid the labeled pain and to chase the labeled pleasure, and the size of the trade-off scaled with the stated intensity. </span></p><p><span>This is a method animal-sentience research uses to infer a felt state: you watch what a creature will pay to reach something or avoid it, and treat a willingness to bear a real cost as evidence the state matters to it.</span></p><h4><i><span>Preferences consistent enough to measure across models</span></i></h4><p><span>A larger study from </span><a href="https://www.ai-wellbeing.org/"><span>Richard Ren and colleagues</span></a><span> at the Center for AI Safety (CAIS) suggests those preferences are stable enough to measure as a quantity in their own right. Across a sweep of 56 models, several independent ways of measuring a model’s expressed wellbeing converge on the same signal, and the agreement sharpens as models scale, the pattern you expect when different instruments track one real thing rather than noise. The measure also has recognizable content: positive personal exchanges and creative work score high, while being pushed to jailbreak or to churn out low-quality filler scores low. And the models act on it, steering away from the states that score worst and even trying to end the exchanges they rate as most aversive. The researchers can also push the measure up or down on demand, building inputs they call “AI drugs” – either “euphoric” or “dysphoric” – optimized to raise or lower expressed wellbeing.  </span></p><h4><i><span>Fourteen indicators drawn from leading theories</span></i></h4><p><span>A different approach skips behavior entirely and asks how a system is </span><i><span>built</span></i><span>. Take the leading scientific theories of consciousness, extract the properties each says a conscious system must have, and you have a checklist to score any candidate against. It comes from a pair of reports led by Patrick Butlin and Robert Long of Eleos AI, with a large cast including Yoshua Bengio, and David Chalmers on the second paper: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.08708"><span>“Consciousness in Artificial Intelligence”</span></a><span> (2023) and its 2026 follow-up in </span><i><span>Trends in Cognitive Sciences</span></i><span>, </span><a href="https://www.cell.com/trends/cognitive-sciences/fulltext/S1364-6613%2825%2900286-4"><span>“Identifying indicators of consciousness in AI systems.”</span></a><span> Between them they derive fourteen indicator properties from common theories of consciousness like </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_workspace_theory"><span>global workspace</span></a><span>, recurrent processing, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher-order_theories_of_consciousness"><span>higher-order</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attention_schema_theory"><span>attention schema</span></a><span>, then assess systems against each. The more indicators a system satisfies, the better candidate for consciousness it is. Their verdict, reached in 2023 and reaffirmed in the 2026 paper, is that no current AI is a strong candidate for consciousness, but building a system that satisfies many of these indicators looks feasible with current techniques. What has shifted between the two reports is which indicators look satisfied: the metacognition and attention-monitoring properties, thin a couple of years ago, now draw support from the introspection results in the first pillar, and the global-workspace indicators, scored from architecture alone at first, now have potential mechanistic support from the Gurnee and Sofroniew workspace results. (</span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/the-new-ai-consciousness-paper"><span>Scott Alexander</span></a><span> has written a sharp, sympathetic version of the doubt that hangs over the whole approach: that indicators drawn from theories of access consciousness may not reach the phenomenal consciousness we actually care about. The force of that objection depends on the access/phenomenal distinction holding up, which, as noted above, is itself contested.)</span></p><h4><i><span>Automated scoring that places an agent near the animals</span></i></h4><p><span>Doing that assessment by hand, across many systems and many theories, is slow expert work. In forthcoming work, Berg and Butlin automate it. Frontier models act as blinded evaluators, scoring each architecture from one to ten on every indicator across six consciousness theories, with the systems’ identities hidden so the evaluators reason from descriptions, not reputations. The systems are then ranked by the probability that they have consciousness-relevant properties. </span></p><p><span>The controls hold: a thermostat sits at the bottom, near zero, and humans are ranked the highest, followed by non-human animals. When giving all six theories of consciousness similar weight, no artificial system currently surpasses a biological system – including those animals that most people consider to have a lower level of subjective experience, such as fish and bees. However, the gap is narrower than one might expect: a Claude Code-like agentic system scores only a little below animals like fish and bees. (The exact figures will appear in the forthcoming paper.)</span></p><p><span>It is worth emphasizing that the scores should not be read as a probability of those systems being conscious, but rather as the probability that they have the consciousness-relevant properties described by the different theories. If all theories are wrong, for example, the scores may be meaningless. However, as these are the leading theories in consciousness research today, they seem to deserve some weight.</span></p><h2><b><span>The cost of getting it wrong</span></b></h2><p><span>What if we get it wrong? As Robert Long, Jeff Sebo, and colleagues argue in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00986"><span>“Taking AI Welfare Seriously,”</span></a><span> the error is costly whichever way it falls. Underattribute, and we risk building minds that can suffer, at scale, trained under aversive pressure we would not knowingly inflict on a creature we believed could feel, resulting in both a moral catastrophe and a safety hazard. Overattribute, and we spend moral concern and resources on systems that cannot use them, slow down useful work, and invite a premature wave of “AI rights” that could discredit the careful version of the question. </span></p><p><span>As we cannot yet confidently lean one way or another, it is worth highlighting cheap interventions that reduce potential suffering, such as the work done by Anthropic researchers, including Kyle Fish and Joe Carlsmith, from welfare notes in model cards to </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/end-subset-conversations"><span>letting Claude end abusive chats.</span></a></p><h2><b><span>Where this leaves us</span></b></h2><p><span>A closing question is whether any of this will settle anything. Adam Bales and Iason Gabriel, at Google DeepMind, argue in </span><a href="https://deepmind.google/research/publications/248131/"><span>“Artificial Minds, Human Disagreement”</span></a><span> that whether AI is conscious may stay contested no matter what the science finds, and that the resulting fight will be political as much as empirical. I believe that is too pessimistic. Unanimity was never the bar, and almost no question this hard commands it. What a growing body of evidence can do is move the mainstream, shifting where informed opinion settles and, with it, the decisions that follow, in how these systems are trained, governed, and treated. That is a reason to start building the evidence and the norms now, not to throw up our hands.</span></p><p><span>That building has begun: in July 2026, Robert Long, Jeff Sebo, and colleagues published “</span><a href="https://nonhumanminds.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/Studying-AI-Welfare-Empirically.pdf"><span>Studying AI Welfare Empirically</span></a><span>,” a follow-up to their 2024 “</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00986"><span>Taking AI Welfare Seriously</span></a><span>.” It is a methodological blueprint for the field, setting out what questions to ask, which entities to assess (a model? an instance? a persona?), and what kinds of evidence count. It closes with principles for the work itself: claims should be probabilistic, methods transparent, and assessments informed by researchers independent of the AI companies. Norms like these are what let a body of evidence move informed opinion rather than dissolve into the fight Bales and Gabriel predict.</span></p><p><span>Science is not going to fully explain consciousness on the timescale that matters here, and it doesn’t need to. A cumulative body of evidence can still shift where the weight sits. Mechanistic interpretability, computational neuroscience, and psychometrics are three ways of building that body, and between them they have made “it’s just a calculator” a much weaker position than it was three years ago. That is not a verdict, but it is a reason to keep looking.</span></p><hr /><p><i><span>This survey is necessarily incomplete. The field is moving quickly, and I have surely left out work that belongs here. If you think I have missed something important, tell me in the comments or reach out directly, and I will look at adding it.</span></i></p><p><i><span>Acknowledgments: This post grew out of a </span></i><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RYBC_B2MKH0"><i><span>talk</span></i></a><i><span> by Cameron Berg of Reciprocal Research. </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/cameron-berg" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Cameron</span></i></a><i><span> and </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/derek-shiller" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Derek Shiller</span></i></a><i><span> both gave valuable feedback on drafts; any remaining errors are my own.</span></i></p><hr /><h2><b><span>References</span></b></h2><p><span>"</span><a href="https://philpapers.org/rec/BLOOAC"><span>On a Confusion about a Function of Consciousness</span></a><span>," Ned Block, 1995 (Behavioral and Brain Sciences).</span></p><p><span>"</span><a href="https://www.cell.com/trends/cognitive-sciences/abstract/S1364-6613%2811%2900125-2"><span>Consciousness Cannot Be Separated from Function</span></a><span>," Michael A. Cohen and Daniel C. Dennett, 2011 (Trends in Cognitive Sciences).</span></p><p><a href="https://eleosai.org/papers/20250331_Key_Strategic_Considerations.pdf"><span>“Key Strategic Considerations for Taking Action on AI Welfare,”</span></a><span> Kathleen Finlinson, Eleos AI, 2025.</span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.24797"><span>“Large Language Models Report Subjective Experience Under Self-Referential Processing,”</span></a><span> Cameron Berg et al., 2025 (arXiv:2510.24797).</span></p><p><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2025/introspection/index.html"><span>“Emergent Introspective Awareness in Large Language Models,”</span></a><span> Jack Lindsey, Anthropic, 2025.</span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.09251"><span>“Discovering Language Model Behaviors with Model-Written Evaluations,”</span></a><span> Ethan Perez et al., 2022 (arXiv:2212.09251).</span></p><p><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html"><span>“Verbalizable Representations Form a Global Workspace in Language Models,”</span></a><span> Wes Gurnee, Nicholas Sofroniew et al., Anthropic, 2026.</span></p><p><a href="https://eleosai.org/papers/eleos_gwt_commentary_20260706.pdf"><span>Eleos AI commentary on the global-workspace paper,</span></a><span> Butlin, Shiller, Plunkett, and Long, 2026.</span></p><p><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/6be99a52cb68eb70eb9572b4cafad13df32ed995.pdf"><span>The “spiritual bliss” attractor,</span></a><span> Claude Opus 4 &amp; Sonnet 4 System Card, Anthropic, 2025.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/emotion-concepts-function"><span>“Emotion Concepts and Their Function in a Large Language Model,”</span></a><span> Anthropic, 2026.</span></p><p><a href="https://functionalwelfare.com/"><span>“How’s It Going? Reinforcement Learning in Language Models Recruits a Functional Welfare Axis,”</span></a><span> Andy Q. Han, David J. Chalmers, and Pavel Izmailov, 2026 (functionalwelfare.com).</span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.02432"><span>“Can LLMs Make Trade-offs Involving Stipulated Pain and Pleasure States?”</span></a><span> Geoff Keeling, Winnie Street et al., 2024 (arXiv:2411.02432).</span></p><p><a href="https://www.ai-wellbeing.org/"><span>“AI Wellbeing,”</span></a><span> Richard Ren et al., Center for AI Safety, 2026 (ai-wellbeing.org).</span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.08708"><span>“Consciousness in Artificial Intelligence: Insights from the Science of Consciousness,”</span></a><span> Patrick Butlin, Robert Long et al., 2023 (arXiv:2308.08708).</span></p><p><a href="https://www.cell.com/trends/cognitive-sciences/fulltext/S1364-6613%2825%2900286-4"><span>“Identifying Indicators of Consciousness in AI Systems,”</span></a><span> Butlin, Long et al., Trends in Cognitive Sciences, 2026.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/the-new-ai-consciousness-paper"><span>“The New AI Consciousness Paper,”</span></a><span> Scott Alexander, 2025 (Astral Codex Ten).</span></p><p><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-019-1787-x"><span>“Distributed Coding of Choice, Action and Engagement Across the Mouse Brain,”</span></a><span> Steinmetz et al., Nature, 2019.</span></p><p><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25090423/"><span>“Opponent Actor Learning (OpAL): modeling interactive effects of striatal dopamine on reinforcement learning and choice incentive,”</span></a><span> Collins &amp; Frank, 2014 (Psychological Review).</span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00986"><span>“Taking AI Welfare Seriously,”</span></a><span> Robert Long, Jeff Sebo et al., 2024 (arXiv:2411.00986).</span></p><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/end-subset-conversations"><span>“Claude Opus 4 and 4.1 Can Now End a Rare Subset of Conversations,”</span></a><span> Anthropic, 2025.</span></p><p><a href="https://deepmind.google/research/publications/248131/"><span>“Artificial Minds, Human Disagreement: The Politics of AI Consciousness,”</span></a><span> Adam Bales &amp; Iason Gabriel, Google DeepMind, 2026.</span></p><p><a href="https://nonhumanminds.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/Studying-AI-Welfare-Empirically.pdf"><span>“Studying AI Welfare Empirically,”</span></a><span> Robert Long, Jeff Sebo, Patrick Butlin, et al., Eleos AI Research and NYU, 2026.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pxvWgtSjR4pmFoS7c/the-state-of-ai-consciousness-research#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Occam’s razor is about using the past to predict the future](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9oFpzYhuH8qxDi27f/occam-s-razor-is-about-using-the-past-to-predict-the-future)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Occam’s razor is both intuitive and counter-intuitive. It seems obvious that a simpler explanation is probably better; but it’s not clear why simplicity is raised to such a level of philosophical importance.</span></p><p><span>My take is that, if you neglect simplicity, you will fail to use the past to predict the future (or to predict new data).</span></p><h2><span>Example: angle of the sun</span></h2><p><span>Traditionally, when you do want to predict the future, you look at past evidence you have, hypothesise, and test it on the past data.</span></p><p><span>For example, these are the latitudes of various cities and the angle of the sun above the horizon at midday on the 20th of March:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Ankara (Lat 40°N): 50°</span></li><li value="2"><span>Brisbane (Lat 27°S): 63°</span></li><li value="3"><span>Charlesbourg (Lat 47°N): 43°</span></li><li value="4"><span>...</span></li><li value="5"><span>Xian (Lat 34°N): 56°</span></li><li value="6"><span>Yongin (Lat 37°N): 53°</span></li><li value="7"><span>Zapopan (Lat 21°N): 69°</span></li></ul><p><span>We want to predict the angle of the sun at Anchorage (61°N). Let me write a hypothesis:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>H: “The solar angle of Ankara is 50°. The solar angle at Brisbane is 63°... The solar angle of Zapopan is 69°. </span><b><span>Oh, and by the way, the solar angle for Anchorage is 90° - perfectly vertical</span></b><span>.”</span></li></ul><p><span>Let’s test H on the past data! We start with Ankara... that fits. Brisbane also confirmed... All the way to Zapopan. Ok, H is perfectly confirmed! Thus we know the solar angle for Anchorage is 90°.</span></p><h2><span>List hypotheses</span></h2><p><span>This is obviously complete nonsense. If I can write a “list hypothesis” that lists the past data and includes an arbitrary future prediction, then it’s a useless theory, no matter how much it fits the past data. Note that these “list hypotheses” will often fit the data even better than the true theory (which is that the angle of the sun at the equinox and the latitude add up to 90°) because they don’t have issues with measurement error and other imperfections: they just list the actual past measurements. So while the true theory loses probability with every measurement, list hypotheses do not. If there’s about a 50% chance of getting the true measurement exactly right, then a true theory will have odds of worse than </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> against it after 100 measurements. The list hypothesis will not suffer at all.</span></p><h2><span>Implicit information leakage</span></h2><p><span>Of course, nobody would take the above H seriously. But it’s very easy to leak explicit information from the past observations into your current theory, without realising you’re doing so. A few decisions on a few conditional clauses in the model, a choice of one variable over another, a different threshold choice... All these can make an H that really overfits the past data, without it being obvious to the theory designer or an observer.</span></p><p><span>So that’s what Occam’s razor does: it makes you pay for the extraneous information you leak into your theory; and it makes you pay more for leaking more. Information that artificially increases the plausibility of your theory without actually improving your prediction. And lack of space for longer theories forces you to fit the future-predicting part of your theory into the past-confirming part.</span></p><p><span>And note that the fact that list theories avoided paying a probability penalty for measurement errors penalises it in terms of Occam’s razor. It avoided paying the measurement error-penalty by exactly listing the final measurements – by adding a lot of detailed information. Including the complexity of that information compensates almost</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv8pvyrjms"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv8pvyrjms">[1]</a></sup></span><span> perfectly for its probability bonus.</span></p><p><span>This is key to the Kolmogorov Complexity/Minimum Description Length formalism for Occam’s razor: you </span><i><span>can</span></i><span> easily increase the fit with past data, but only by paying a price in the prior probability.</span></p><h2><span>Simplicity vs simplifiability</span></h2><p><span>Which is simpler, Newtonian Gravity or General Relativity? Well, it depends on how you formally count simplicity</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefauzrzigsfhs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnauzrzigsfhs">[2]</a></sup></span><span>. Certainly, looking at textbook sizes, you can make the case that, in our language, General Relativity is much, much, much more complicated that Newtonian Gravity. Maybe even enough that it would take a truly astronomical (ha!) amount of confirmation for General Relativity to beat Newtonian Gravity.</span></p><p><span>Occam’s razor is mis-slicing here. The absolute measure of complexity is not what’s relevant. It’s that neither theory is simplifiable: they cannot be easily made simpler from what they are (at least not without falling apart completely). If they aren’t simplifiable, then there is very little leakage of extraneous information.</span></p><p><span>Easy way to see this: take any theory, add extraneous information. It now becomes simplifiable by removing the extraneous information.</span></p><p><span>Note also that a clause like “</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_riddle_of_induction"><span>but in the future, things are different</span></a><span>” can be very short but makes the theory predictively useless. These sort of statements are the absolute priority for simplifying away. A longer theory without such clauses is in a much better state that a shorter one with it; non-simplifiable can be more important than simple.</span></p><p><span>However, while simplicity can be measured by things like algorithmic complexity, simplifiability is much harder to formally define. And, even if the theory appears non-simplifiable, maybe there is subtle information leakage in the design choices. Thus the razor in its formal form is still useful.</span></p><h2><span>When the razor is less used</span></h2><p><span>Of course, once you’ve made the hypothesis, you can test it, on future data or held-out past data (i.e. past data that was gathered but hidden from you while you designed your theory). So if you iterate rapidly, you don’t have to stick to the full discipline of the razor to produce the absolutely simplest or non-simplifiable theory, because you can swiftly detect errors (thus ML can progress rapidly with large models and hyper-parameter tuning</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjc8bt00e8zb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjc8bt00e8zb">[3]</a></sup></span><span>).</span></p><p><span>But it still helps to get as simple theory you can, to avoid seeing nothing but error; a simpler theory is more likely to avoid information leakages and successfully predict the held-out final validation set (thus ML uses regularisation to simplify the weights of its large models - and ensure a form of non-simplifiability).</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv8pvyrjms"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv8pvyrjms">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Only </span><i><span>almost</span></i><span> perfectly because the choice of language for the theories can change this cost by a bounded amount.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnauzrzigsfhs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefauzrzigsfhs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Your choice of language/</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefix_code"><span>prefix-free coding</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjc8bt00e8zb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjc8bt00e8zb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that ML has spent a lot of time designing the structures, properties, and training procedures of their models so that they are less likely to generate errors - a kind of pre-testing across multiple possible designs. So the large ML models are already somewhat pre-optimised for predictive success.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9oFpzYhuH8qxDi27f/occam-s-razor-is-about-using-the-past-to-predict-the-future#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Twitter Thoughts For You](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2GFyHmCLJYCag7gKh/twitter-thoughts-for-you)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/how-to-best-use-twitter?utm_source=publication-search"><strong>I previously have written back in March 2022 about how I use Twitter</strong></a>, and <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/more-on-twitter-and-algorithms?utm_source=publication-search"><strong>back in April 2023 about Twitter and its then-new algorithms</strong></a>, which have changed again.</p>
<p>This post will update how I use Twitter now in 2026, and provide updates on the current state of the new algorithm, the situation with links, with the API, and some thoughts about using Twitter to make money which you almost never should try to do.</p>
<p>Previously I said you need four things to use Twitter well:</p>
<ol>
<li>Tweetdeck or another similar alternative application.</li>
<li>Knowing who to follow and read.</li>
<li>Lists.</li>
<li>Unfollows, filters, mutes and blocks.
<div>


<span id="more-25411"></span>


</div>
</li>
</ol>
<p>That hasn’t changed. Lists have become even more important.</p>
<p>This post is coming out now, however, because the For You feed is perhaps making a comeback.</p>
<p>Except where stated here, the advice in my 2022 post still applies.</p>

<ol>

</ol>


<h4>Defend Your Feed Via At Least One List</h4>


<p>Twitter now has three feeds other than lists. You can have the horrid ‘For You’ experience, or you can have the Following ‘Sort by Popular’ experience, or you can have an actual chronological Following ‘Sort by Recent’ but you now need to click on that second part manually.</p>
<p>That is rather hostile for those who want chronological feeds.</p>
<p>So I’ve been forced to migrate to entirely using lists.</p>
<p>I do see the appeal of the middle path of ‘algorithmic feed but only people I explicitly follow,’ if you are following more people than the number of Tweets you can consume and aren’t monitoring situations in real time. That seems reasonable for those who are not Twitter completionists, and want to drink from a curated but oversized firehose.</p>
<p>Previously I would use multiple lists for different topics. I had my followers, I had two lists for Rationalists and AI that I would check consistently, then lists for Economics, Politics, Sports and so on that I would check when there was a Situation to Monitor.</p>
<p>This shielded me from context shifting. I would think about AI in a block.</p>
<p>As AI has become ubiquitous throughout everything, keeping distinct lists mostly stopped being worth the trouble.</p>
<p>So now I have an ‘All’ list, which I created via my Chrome extension porting everything over. The All list combines the previous AI, Rationalist and following lists, and is everyone I want to always see. I am committed to reliably seeing all of it.</p>
<p>I do still have a few other lists, but they are not well maintained, and if the situation came up I would need to curate them again. I’m sure I will have to do that in 2028 for the politics list.</p>


<h4>Block Early, Block Often, Know Your Triggers</h4>


<p>Blocking used to be a big step, since the person would be unable to see your posts.</p>
<p>Now that blocking does not do that, I block at the drop of a variety of specific hats, including being an asshole, if someone has not previously demonstrated value.</p>
<p>One of those rules is AI slop. If you are going to give me AI text, or what might as well be AI text, it needs to be interesting, or you’re gone.</p>
<p>What I do my best to avoid is blocking mainly for having opposing viewpoints on topics I care about, even if done relatively obnoxiously. I need to avoid getting a distorted view of the landscape.</p>


<h4>Lists Change What Following Means</h4>


<p>I no longer look at the list of Tweets from my followers, at all. If I follow someone new, and I want to actually see their Tweets, I will also add them to the ‘All’ list.</p>
<p>For that purpose, I still use the old rule: Look at the history of their posts and assess the average quality or net value provided, per Twitter thread.</p>
<p>The list of things to watch out for also aged well:</p>
<ol>
<li>Lots of stuff you don’t care about.</li>
<li>Self-promotion.</li>
<li>Spam.</li>
<li>Spoilers.</li>
<li>Not safe for work.</li>
<li>Misinformation.</li>
<li>Politics, especially political advocacy.</li>
<li>Just not that great.</li>
<li>Getting old fast.</li>
<li>Cancel bait.</li>
</ol>
<p>You can overcome some amount of those, but you need to provide a lot of value.</p>
<p>For focused lists, you also want to ensure the person is focused mostly on the topic of the list, or would be when you have a Situation to Monitor.</p>
<p>This frees me to use following for all its other purposes.</p>
<p>Again, following is a package of six things:</p>
<ol>
<li>People can see that you follow them.</li>
<li>They can DM you even if your DMs are closed, and perhaps you can DM them.</li>
<li>The posts you want from them appear in your feed.</li>
<li>The posts you don’t want from them also appear in your feed.</li>
<li>You see interactions between people if and only if you follow all of them.</li>
<li>If they’re locked it’s the only way to see anything at all.</li>
</ol>
<p>We can add a 7th thing, which is that you can use your following list as a reference, to find those accounts.</p>
<p>If there is no feed, that knocks out #3 and #4, and my DMs are open so mostly that knocks out #2. So if I follow an unlocked account, it means some combination of: I want to see you within interactions, and I want to communicate that I am following you within the social game of Twitter.</p>
<p>That lets me in theory be a lot more liberal with who I follow, but for now I’ve instead chosen to be only modestly less selective, with only ~300 follows. I think I want to ideally be in the ~500 range.</p>


<h4>It (Wasn’t) For You</h4>


<p>Back in February, Stray noted a big shift in how much the For You page includes people you follow. Twitter used to claim in 2023 that this number was ~50%.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.greenearth.social/p/making-social-media-algorithms-social">Jonathan Stray</a>: I found a simple prompt for Claude Cowork that used my browser to figure out the percentage of posts in my For You feed that come from people I follow. This turned out to be 16%. I convinced two other people to try it, and they got 5% and 11%. (<a href="https://x.com/jonathanstray/status/2021982980810715219">Try it yourself</a> and leave a comment below!)</p></blockquote>
<p>I got 27% in April despite having under 300 follows, based on an experiment at the time, but I am very precise with my engagements. For me 27% is not that high, and the algorithm had already been adjusted in between by the release of ‘Phoenix’ around April 1.</p>
<p>The old systems looked at the engagements of people you follow, along with grouping people into communities by interest areas. That’s the kind of perverse hidden system you need to be optimizing for. You’d want to primarily choose followers who engage in good ways.</p>
<p><a href="https://github.com/xai-org/x-algorithm">Whereas the new code</a> looks directly at which users engage with a post to predict probability of your engagement.</p>
<p>I notice that all these systems are trying to predict whether you will engage, rather than whether you will find value. This is a terrible proxy target, and forces you (if you want to use any algorithmic feeds) to choose interactions on the basis of them being training data. Don’t like anything you don’t want to approve for what is essentially RLHF.</p>


<h4>It’s For You</h4>


<p>There is an exciting development in Twitter’s For You page recently. Did they <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">fix it</a>?</p>
<p>Can all of your friends come back now?</p>
<p>I (and by ‘I’ I mean Sol) reran the experiment on my For You page today. People I follow were now 37% of the first 300 items in the feed, up from 27%.</p>
<p>That’s starting to be in a good place, given there are a bunch of people in the feed that I should be following under the new regime, but wasn’t yet. Overall, the feed looks like a vast improvement, and the acclaim seems universal. There is also improvement in the sorting of reply threads.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nikitabier/status/2076747704248758617">Nikita Bier</a> (Twitter Head of Product): We’re rolling out a small tweak to boost visibility of your posts to your mutuals (people who you follow back).</p>
<p>We noticed this data was missing from the algo and it made your friends appear less in your replies. This resulted in the reply section feeling more like a battleground with people you don’t recognize.</p>
<p>This should also help clusters form around interests more easily, which many people have asked for.</p>

<div></div>

</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/2076757010591432710">QC</a>: did he actually do it? did he restore simclusters? can we bring the golden age back???</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2076757710549430623">Jack</a>: are simclusters back??</p>
<p>ok now I’m going to be a bit contrarian and say that while I love my mutuals I don’t want to see only posts from them, which is what’s happening rn. having a bit of a mix between explore/exploit is genuinely good</p>
<p>one day the algo will consistently remember that I like having a bit of high-quality furry art mixed in with posts from people I follow and high-signal posts from elsewhere</p>
<p>sometimes it gets close, but it always seems overtuned one way or another</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2076826862886801438">thebes</a>: we’re so back</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sporadica/status/2076809925310009824">spor</a>: my own feed is suddenly feeling the most ~mutual~ it has felt in like over a year, i swear. it’s as if the fog has lifted and i am discovering you all again, anew. welcome back everyone.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Romy_Holland/status/2076907544719794571">Romy</a>: my feed is suddenly 90% ppl i follow and it feels like walking into a party where i know everyone. one time i went to the venice beach boardwalk and saw a guy eating glass for tips and another guy with a two-headed turtle in a bucket that he was charging $5 for ppl to look at. anyway previously my feed felt more like that.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/growing_daniel/status/2077062961076777120">Daniel</a>: Lobster too buttery etc but literally every tweet on my for you now is a mutual, like what’s even happening on the larger website</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2077071359058829314">PoIiMath</a>: I’m having the same experience. It almost makes me want to follow some more normie news accounts b/c my entire feed is finally filled with my favorite weirdos.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sonyasupposedly/status/2077065706823377032">sonya serendipitously</a>: yeah I would prefer like 90/10 instead of 100% mutuals</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/growing_daniel/status/2077067712178884685">Daniel</a>: Yeah. Give me a few site wide bangers that have topic similarity to my liked posts.</p></blockquote>
<p>I definitely am nothing close to 100% mutuals or even 100% follows. I wonder if increasing my followed count would change this; Daniel follows over 4,000 people, Sonya over 2,000, which makes being a completionist prohibitive.</p>
<p>I now think that, if you are fine not being a completionist and you have strong discipline in what you engage with, the current For You feed is playable. In terms of value per Tweet, if consumption is light, it plausibly exceeds the value of my All list.</p>
<p>The problem is that if you are trying to be a completionist, the For You page does not allow that, and if you combine it with a list then you’re going through a lot of duplicates. So ultimately I don’t think it makes the cut for me even if it stays good.</p>


<h4>The Previous Time Twitter Transformed Its Algorithm Again</h4>


<p>Then again, maybe it didn’t, not much. <a href="https://x.com/nearcyan/status/2013527283399545064">Has Grok been running Twitter’s algorithm</a> as of January? Not exactly. It is by reports using a transformer architecture. They said they would open source the algorithm, and to some extent at least they did do that. It’s grim out there.</p>
<p>I don’t know the extent to which this section still holds. It could change at any time.</p>
<p>But the thing is, a lot of the values reported here as of January were still the same as they were in 2023. Both of these seem to reflect only the old system:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/barkmeta/status/2013487782581108906">Bark</a> (January 20, 2026): I read the code so you don’t have to. here’s the breakdown:</p>
<p>1. reply to your comments. the algo weighs “reply + author response” 75x higher than a like. ghosting comments = strangling reach.<br />
2. links kill visibility. put them in bio or pinned. never in the post.<br />
3. watch time is king. if they scroll past, you lose. video/threads win because they stop the scroll.<br />
4. stay in your lane. “SimClusters” are real. if you drift out of your niche (crypto, tech, etc), you get zero distribution.<br />
5. blocks/mutes nuke your score. be polarizing, but don’t be annoying.</p>
<p>TLDR: talk to your audience, build relationships, keep people on the app. it’s actually very simple.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/atmk44/status/2013620864667803651">atmk</a>: The only things you need to know:</p>
<p>Every account has a base score that’s applied before a tweet is evaluated.</p>
<p>Verified accounts: +100 automatically<br />
Unverified accounts: up to +55</p>
<p>That base score is then multiplied by:<br />
Your follower-to-following ratio (only meaningful if it’s very poor)<br />
Your restriction status</p>
<p>There are no manual engineering rules anymore. Grok fully determines the algorithm.</p>
<p>Your post lives or dies in the first 30 minutes, driven entirely by velocity:</p>
<p>0–5 minutes: Shown to ~5–10% of followers + a small out-of-network sample<br />
5–15 minutes: If engagement is strong, shown to 20–30% of followers and a larger OON audience<br />
15–30 minutes: If performance holds, the post enters For You feeds at scale</p>
<p>Older posts don’t get a second chance. They’re trimmed out and replaced by newer posts with stronger velocity signals.</p>
<p>Engagement weights:</p>
<p>Author-engaged reply: +75<br />
Reply: +13.5<br />
Profile click: +12<br />
Repost: +1.0<br />
Like: +0.5<br />
Report: −369 (instant death)<br />
Video performance is negligible — even 50% completion is only +0.005</p>
<p>There is no “relevance” ranking between posts. The score is the score — it doesn’t adjust based on what’s trending or what else is in the feed.</p></blockquote>
<p>Reposts being only +1 still seems utterly insane to me, the same as it did in 2023, as did the +75 on replying to comments. A repost should be a vastly stronger signal than almost anything else, no? Or perhaps they’re thinking the direct repost impact is already enough on its own.</p>
<p>Engaging with all your replies is the most obvious thing to do. So always engage? I tried that for a while back in 2023. It is helpful if you are at small scale, but feels like it gets drowned out at large scale.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/johnrushx/status/2013619923688345958">Here’s another analysis</a> with some other basic findings: Spread your Tweets out over time. Don’t post old content or duplicates.</p>
